The task: prove strong normalization for STLC CBV with locally nameless. We need a unary logical relation.

Key challenges:
1. The typing rule T_Abs uses `open t1 (tm_fvar x)` with a cofinite quantification over L.
2. `step` relation: ST_AppAbs requires locally_closed (tm_abs T t) and value v.
3. `has_type` has no locally-closed premise, but T_Abs's body is well-typed under a context; fvar-based opening.

Standard approach: Define a logical relation on terms indexed by type:
- For Bool: SN t (or maybe SN and something).
- For arrow T1 -> T2: SN t and for all v (or all terms u) with LR T1 u and value v... Actually with CBV, define:
  LR (T1 -> T2) t := SN t /\ forall u, value u -> LR T1 u -> LR T2 (tm_app t u).
- LR Bool t := SN t.

But then fundamental theorem: for a well-typed term with free variables in context, we need an interpretation of contexts where each variable x : T gets a value-related term (a substitution). In locally nameless with fvar atoms, we can use an "environment" mapping atoms to closed values? Actually substitution for free variables can be arbitrary terms satisfying the LR. The standard "semantic typing" via the "substitution" approach: define for each context Gamma a set of substitutions sigma (functions atom -> tm) such that for each x with Gamma x = Some T, LR T (sigma x); and for x not in Gamma, sigma x = tm_fvar x? Hmm, but free variables not in context can appear? No — well-typed terms only use free variables via T_Var, which requires Gamma x = Some T. But T_Abs opens with a fresh fvar x and extends context. So in the fundamental theorem, we handle abs by choosing fresh x and extending the environment.

Actually the standard trick with locally nameless and fvar is to prove:

Lemma fundamental : forall Gamma t T, has_type Gamma t T -> forall sigma, (forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> LR T (sigma x)) -> LR T (subst sigma t)?

Hmm, but substitution over tm with fvar: we can define subst (sigma : atom -> tm) : tm -> tm replacing fvar x by sigma x. Then the fundamental theorem instantiates sigma = identity (tm_fvar), giving LR T t, from which SN t. But we need context sem: for empty context, trivially.

In CBV weak normalization proofs, the logical relation usually is defined on terms with LR (arrow) t := SN t /\ forall t2, LR T1 t2 -> LR T2 (app t t2) — quantification over all arguments, not just values. But then the proof of backward closure for app uses ST_App2: if t2 --> t2', app v1 t2 --> app v1 t2'. And ST_App1: app t1 t2 --> app t1' t2 requires t1 --> t1' and locally_closed t2. Let me think about the right LR definition for CBV strong normalization. Standard: 

For CBV, neutral terms and values. A common definition:
- LR Bool t := SN t.
- LR (A -> B) t := SN t /\ forall u, value u -> LR A u -> LR B (app t u).

Then fundamental theorem: at app, we need to show LR T2 (app t1 t2) given LR (T1->T2) t1 and LR T1 t2. Since t2 may not be value, we can't directly use the second component of LR arrow. Key insight for CBV: define LR on values and terms separately? A "value relation" VR T v for values, and LR T t for terms = SN t /\ (forall reducts...) with the property that every LR term either is a VR value or steps to LR terms.

Standard Tait-style for CBV (e.g., from Harper's PFPL): Define the "closed values of type T" and "closed computations" via:
- V[Bool] = {true,false}
- V[A->B] = {λx.e | forall v ∈ V[A], e[v/x] ∈ C[B]}
- C[T] = {e | e -->* v with v ∈ V[T]}  (for CBV, terms that evaluate to values) — but that's semantic evaluation, and strong normalization would follow if all C terms SN.

Actually the simplest logical relation for CBV normalization is often defined as:

LR T t := SN t and (t is a value -> VR T t)

where VR is defined recursively:
- VR Bool v := v = true or v = false.
- VR (A->B) (abs s) := forall v, VR A v -> LR B (app (abs s) v)? or LR B (open s v)?

  - SN: t1 is SN and t2 is SN. Need a lemma: SN t1 -> LR A t2 (= SN t2 + ...) -> SN (app t1 t2) for t1 of function type. Hmm, this is essentially the crux. With t2 arbitrary (not necessarily value), we can prove by induction on SN t1 and SN t2 combined:
    Reducts of app t1 t2:

OK here's a cleaner plan inspired by standard CBV reducibility:

Define two relations simultaneously? In Coq, we can define LR by recursion on type with a single relation that includes both a "term" and "value" component:

```
Fixpoint LR (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => SN t /\ (value t -> t = tm_true \/ t = tm_false)
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => 
      SN t /\
      (value t -> forall v, value v -> LR T1 v -> LR T2 (app t v)) /\
      (forall v, value v -> LR T1 v -> LR T2 (app t v))
  end.
```

Let's now design the environment semantics and substitution.

Definitions:

```
Definition subst (sigma : atom -> tm) : tm -> tm :=
  fix subst_rec t :=
    match t with
    | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
    | tm_fvar x => sigma x
    | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst_rec t1) (subst_rec t2)
    | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst_rec t1)
    | tm_true => tm_true
    | tm_false => tm_false
    end.
```

Note: with locally nameless, bvars remain; subst sigma t may have bvars if t does; but well-typed t won't.

Opening and substitution commute: 
`subst sigma (open t u) = open (subst sigma t) (subst sigma u)`? Check: open_rec k u t replaces bvar k occurrences; subst replaces fvars. Since disjoint, yes. Formally:
Lemma subst_open_rec: forall sigma t u k, subst sigma (open_rec k u t) = open_rec k (subst sigma u) (subst sigma t).
Proof by induction on t, generalizing k. For bvar i: if i =? k then subst sigma u = open_rec k (subst sigma u) (bvar i) since i=k... yes RHS open_rec k w (bvar k) = w. Good. For fvar x: sigma x both sides. abs: IH with S k. Fine.

But careful with σ on u: in `subst sigma (open_rec k u t)` the u substituted is not further substituted at the point of insertion... Actually subst sigma (open t u) = open (subst sigma t) (subst sigma u)? In the RHS, `open_rec k (subst sigma u) (subst sigma t)`: when the open inserts subst sigma u at bvar k positions, then no further substitution inside it. On the LHS, subst sigma traverses open_rec output: at a bvar k position, output is u, then subst sigma continues into... wait subst is defined by recursion on t and applied after open_rec finished; subst sigma (open_rec k u t) will traverse the inserted u and substitute inside it too. Yes both sides substitute inside u: RHS substitutes u before insertion. Same result. Good.

Also composition: subst sigma (subst tau t) = subst (fun x => subst sigma (tau x)) t. Standard.

Environment semantics:
```
Definition env_sem (Gamma : context) (sigma : atom -> tm) : Prop :=
  forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> LR T (sigma x).
```
Hmm, but we also need: for atoms not in Gamma (or all atoms), does sigma x need anything? In the T_Abs case, we choose fresh x and extend sigma at x. For the "other atoms" we use the same sigma. The premise of T_Abs is for all x not in L. The context is x |-> T1 ; Gamma = update Gamma x T1. env_sem of that with sigma' requires for all y T, update Gamma x T1 y = Some T -> LR T (sigma' y). For y = x: sigma' x = v, need LR T1 v — given. For y ≠ x: sigma' y = sigma y, need Gamma y = Some T -> LR T (sigma y) — from env_sem Gamma sigma. Good. We need the update lookup lemmas.

But we also need: x fresh not in FV(t1) so that subst sigma' t1 = subst sigma t1 and more importantly subst sigma' (open t1 (fvar x)) = open s v where s = subst sigma t1. Let's verify:
subst sigma' (open t1 (fvar x)) = open (subst sigma' t1) (subst sigma' (fvar x)) = open (subst sigma' t1) v. If x ∉ FV(t1), subst sigma' t1 = subst sigma t1 = s. So = open s v. 

But hold on: in the T_Abs fundamental case, s = subst sigma t1 where sigma is the outer environment, and open s v = open (subst sigma t1) v. Freshness: we need x not in L (from typing premise) and x not free in t1. Since both finite sets, pick fresh. Also we might need x not equal to anything else? In sigma' = update sigma x v. And need subst sigma' t1 = subst sigma t1 requires x ∉ FV(t1). Yes.

Define FV:
```
Fixpoint fv (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => []
  | tm_fvar x => [x]
  | tm_app a b => fv a ++ fv b
  | tm_abs _ b => fv b
  | _ => []
  end.
```
Lemma: x ∉ fv t -> subst (update sigma x v) t = subst sigma t. Need a general lemma with lookup on fvars; prove by induction: for fvar y: if y=x (requires x ∈ fv t, contradiction) else update sigma x v y = sigma y. Hmm, if y = x then fv = [x], contradicts not-in. So y ≠ x, update returns sigma y (Nat.eqb x y = false). Good.

Fresh atom existence: define `fresh (l : list atom) : atom := S (fold_right max 0 l)`? Need lemma. Or simpler: use `max_list`? Let's prove:

```
Fixpoint max_list (l : list atom) : atom :=
  match l with
  | [] => 0
  | x :: xs => Nat.max x (max_list xs)
  end.

Lemma in_max_list : forall l x, In x l -> x <= max_list l.
Lemma fresh_not_in : forall l, ~ In (S (max_list l)) l.
```

Then given L and t1, pick x = S (max_list (L ++ fv t1)); then x ∉ L and x ∉ fv t1, plus x ∉ fv... wait we also need x fresh in something else? In T_Abs premise we need `~ In x L`. Good. Do we need x not in the domain of something? update is total, no.

Now, LR definition. Let's decide the exact definition to make everything go through.

Option A:
```
Fixpoint LR (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=

(without lc). Then ST_App1 requires lc t2 — in forward closure at arrow we need lc t2 from LR T1 t2 — not available. Could add lc as conjunct:

Lemma fundamental : forall Gamma t T, has_type Gamma t T ->

Induction on has_type derivation, generalizing sigma.

Let's try to prove this lemma by induction on the SN derivation of u and on type B? Or maybe by induction on the structure of the SN of u, using the stuck/value/step trichotomy, and handling the arrow case with a nested lemma. Let's think.

Claim: `LR_app : forall A B t u, LR (A->B) t -> LR A u -> LR B (app t u)`.

Proof attempt by induction on SN u (the derivation), i.e., strong induction: LR A u gives SN u; we can do `induction 1` on the SN derivation? In Coq, `strongly_normalizing` is an inductive with one constructor SN_intro: forall t, (forall t', t-->t' -> SN t') -> SN t. Induction on SN u gives IH: forall u', u --> u' -> (LR A u' -> LR B (app t u'))? Hmm, the induction hypothesis would be for the *premise* function? Actually induction on a proof of SN u where u is a variable: the constructor argument is a function, and Coq's induction principle for strongly_normalizing:

```
strongly_normalizing_ind :
  forall P : tm -> Prop,
  (forall t, (forall t', step t t' -> strongly_normalizing t') ->
             (forall t', step t t' -> P t') -> P t) ->
  forall t, strongly_normalizing t -> P t
```
Hmm, actually since the premise `(forall t', t --> t' -> SN t')` is a function type, the induction principle gives IH for each t' with a step: P t'. So we get: to prove P u, assume for all u' with u-->u', P u'. Then prove P u. But we need P u' for LR A u' — we need forward closure LR A u' from LR A u and u --> u'. That's provable by induction on A (structural).

So `induction on SN u` with P u := LR A u -> LR B (app t u) (for fixed t, A, B with LR (A->B) t). Wait, the IH would be P u' for u' a reduct of u. Then in the proof for u, cases on whether u is a value, steps, or stuck:
- If u is a value: LR B (app t u): use t's arrow component directly! Yes! Since t LR arrow and u value LR A u, we get LR B (app t u). Done, no induction needed.
- If u is not a value and steps to u': forward closure gives LR A u' and IH on u' gives LR B (app t u'). And SN(app t u) follows? We need LR B (app t u) which includes SN(app t u). Hmm, P u is LR B (app t u); given LR B (app t u') for all reducts u', can we conclude LR B (app t u)? The term app t u has reducts: 
    * ST_App1: t --> t', lc u: app t' u. Is that LR B? We know LR (A->B) t and t --> t' gives... forward closure at arrow type: LR B (app t' u) would require arrow component of t' and LR A u. Forward closure gives LR (A->B) t', so arrow component gives LR B (app t' u) — but t' might be a value? Arrow component requires u value! Ugh: LR arrow t' = SN t' /\ lc t' /\ forall v, value v -> LR A v -> LR B (app t' v). u is not a value in this case. Hmm, so app t' u isn't covered.
    
    But wait, if u is not a value and u steps, t could also step or be a value. Let's see: We want LR B (app t u) from all reducts being LR B. Case on t:

Let me look at this from the "reducibility candidates" literature for CBV. In CBV, the standard reducibility candidate definition (e.g., in "A semantic analysis of control operators" or Pitts' lectures):

A reducibility candidate is a set of terms closed under:
- if t ∈ R then SN(t);
- if t reduces to t' and t' ∈ R then t ∈ R (backward closure);
- if t is a value and ... 

For CBV, one defines "computable" sets of values C(A) and terms? Let me recall the standard proof of CBV strong normalization:

Define for each type A a set R_A of terms:
- R_Bool = SN.
- R_{A→B} = { t | SN(t) ∧ ∀v ∈ V_A. t v ∈ R_B }, where V_A = R_A ∩ Values.

And the key lemma: if t ∈ R_{A→B} and u ∈ R_A then t u ∈ R_B. Proof is NOT direct; you need t's structure. Hmm, in many presentations they prove this as the "fundamental theorem" plus a "closure under application" lemma proved simultaneously with SN.

VR (value relation):
- VR Bool v := v = true ∨ v = false.
- VR (A->B) (abs s) := forall v, value v -> LR A v -> LR B (open s v). (Or: forall v, VR A v -> LR B (open s v)?)

Actually the common "reducibility candidates" definition for CBV (from e.g. "Normalization by Evaluation" literature or Pitts) is:

A reducibility candidate R is a set of terms such that:
(R1) R ⊆ SN
(R2) if t ∈ R and t --> t' then t' ∈ R (closure under reduction)
(R3) if t is neutral (not a value) and all its reducts are in R then t ∈ R (backward closure for neutral terms)

Then define:
- R_Bool = SN (or {t | all reducts are true/false...}) hmm.
- R_{A->B} = { t | SN t ∧ ∀v ∈ V_A, t v ∈ R_B } where V_A = values ∩ R_A.

And the key lemmas:
1. R_T ⊆ SN.
2. If t ∈ R_T and t --> t' then t' ∈ R_T.
3. If t neutral and ∀t' t-->t' → t' ∈ R_T then t ∈ R_T.
4. If t ∈ R_{A->B} and u ∈ R_A then t u ∈ R_B. (the crucial one)
5. If v ∈ R_A and v value then v ∈ V_A etc.

Lemma 4's proof: t ∈ R_{A->B}. Need t u ∈ R_B. Since R_B ⊆ SN, need SN(t u). Standard proof: by induction on SN(t) and SN(u)? And using property (R3) for R_B: if t u is neutral and all its reducts are in R_B then done. Reducts of t u:
  - t --> t': t' u. t' ∈ R_{A->B} by (R2) at arrow. Then by... induction on SN t (t' is a reduct) gives t' u ∈ R_B.
  - u --> u' (t value): t u'. u' ∈ R_A by (R2). Then by induction on SN u (u' is a reduct) gives t u' ∈ R_B.
  - t = abs s value, u = v value: beta → open s v ∈ R_B. From VR definition of abs s: since s's value relation says forall v ∈ V_A, open s v... wait VR (A->B) (abs s) := forall v, value v -> LR A v -> LR B (open s v). And t ∈ R_{A->B} gives t ∈ SN and the "forall v ∈ V_A, t v ∈ R_B"; for t = abs s value, is that equivalent to VR? We need R_{A->B} to imply VR on values. Hmm, if R_{A->B} is defined as {SN t ∧ forall v ∈ V_A, t v ∈ R_B}, then for t = abs s value and v ∈ V_A: t v ∈ R_B. But beta gives t v --> open s v; (R2) doesn't turn t v ∈ R_B into open s v ∈ R_B — that's forward direction, yes! t v ∈ R_B and t v --> open s v gives open s v ∈ R_B by (R2) at B. Great, so beta case uses forward closure of R_B applied to t v ∈ R_B (which comes from t's definition and v ∈ V_A). 

So the structure works if R_B satisfies (R2) forward closure. And (R3) neutral backward closure. This avoids needing "value relation" separately: R_{A->B} t := SN t ∧ forall v, value v -> R_A v -> R_B (t v). Wait, this is exactly the Option A definition (with value premise)! And the application lemma and backward closure are proven using induction on SN derivations plus properties of R. Let me now try to formalize this properly.

Definition:
```
Fixpoint LR (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => SN t
  | Ty_Arrow A B => SN t /\ forall v, value v -> LR A v -> LR B (app t v)
  end.
```
Note: LR arrow includes SN. (Does it need lc? For ST_App1 in forward closure: t --> t' gives app t v --> app t' v requiring locally_closed v! We need lc v from LR A v. Hmm. Add lc to LR? Let's add `lc t` as a conjunct at every type:
```
LR Bool t := SN t /\ lc t
LR (A->B) t := SN t /\ lc t /\ forall v, value v -> LR A v -> LR B (app t v)
```
Then LR A v -> lc v. Good.)

Properties to prove by induction on T:
(P1) LR T t -> SN t. (conjunct)
(P2) LR T t -> lc t. (conjunct)
(P3) Forward closure: LR T t -> t --> t' -> LR T t'.
   Induction on T.
   - Bool: SN forward: need lemma SN_step: SN t -> t-->t' -> SN t'. That's direct by inversion on SN t. lc forward: need lc_step: lc t -> t --> t' -> lc t'. Hmm! Does step preserve lc? ST_AppAbs: app (abs T s) v --> open s v; lc (app (abs s) v) gives lc_at 0 (abs T s) = lc_at 1 s, and lc v. Need lc (open s v) = lc_at 0 (open_rec 0 v s). This is the standard open-lc lemma: lc_at 1 s -> lc_at 0 v -> lc_at 0 (open_rec 0 v s). Yes provable. ST_App1: app t1 t2 --> app t1' t2 with lc t2 and lc t1 -> lc t1'; lc(app) from lc t1', lc t2. ST_App2 similar. So lc_step provable.
   - Arrow: LR T t' requires SN t', lc t', forall v value LR A v -> LR B (app t' v). We have SN t' from SN t via SN_step; lc t' from lc_step; and app t v --> app t' v via ST_App1 with lc v (from LR A v); LR B (app t v) from t's arrow component; forward closure at B (IH on T= A->B's component B, structural subterm) gives LR B (app t' v). Good.
(P4) Backward closure for neutral terms: if lc t, not value t, SN t, and forall t', t --> t' -> LR T t', then LR T t.
   Wait, do we need lc t? For the arrow component: LR (A->B) t needs SN t, lc t, and forall v value LR A v -> LR B (app t v). To show LR B (app t v), we want to use backward closure at B (IH on B): need lc (app t v) (have from lc t, lc v), not value (app t v) (true, apps are never values), SN (app t v), and all reducts LR B.
   Reducts of app t v:
     - ST_App1 with t --> t' and lc v: app t' v; hypothesis gives LR (A->B) t' so arrow component gives LR B (app t' v) (v value, LR A v). ✓.
     - ST_App2 requires value t: but t is not a value. ✗.
     - ST_AppAbs requires t = abs: then t would be value if lc (abs)... wait value (tm_abs T s) requires locally_closed (tm_abs T s) which we have from lc t. So t = abs would make t a value, contradiction. ✗.
   So all reducts are LR B. SN (app t v): need SN of app given SN t, SN v, all reducts SN. We can prove SN(app t v) by SN_intro: for each reduct, SN. The reducts are app t' v with LR B → SN. But wait SN_intro for app t v requires all `app t v --> w` have SN w; we have w = app t' v and LR B (app t' v) → SN. ✓. lc (app t v): lc t, lc v. Not value: app not value. Then IH at B gives LR B (app t v). ✓.
   So (P4) works! Note we need `SN t` as hypothesis? We used SN t to... actually in the arrow component proof we didn't use SN t except as a conjunct to prove. We get SN t from hypothesis. Fine. And in the base case Bool: LR Bool t = SN t ∧ lc t, given.
   
   Actually (P4) as stated needs the "all reducts LR" hypothesis for LR T but only uses it for the function component. Good.

(P5) Application lemma: LR (A->B) t -> LR A u -> LR B (app t u).
   Proof: We have LR A u → SN u, lc u, value-or-neutral.

Standard: `strongly_normalizing_ind` gives the IH for reducts. We can use `induction Hsn` directly if the goal is stated with Hsn as a variable and t generalized appropriately. Coq handles `induction Hsn as [t Hred IH]` where Hsn : SN t and the goal may depend on t; it will revert the goal and t automatically. If Ht : LR (A->B) t is also in the context and depends on t, Coq needs to generalize it. Typically `induction Hsn` will fail or produce a weaker IH if Ht mentions t. P4: forall T t, lc t -> ~ value t -> strongly_normalizing t -> (forall t', t --> t' -> LR T t') -> LR T t.

SN_step: SN t -> t --> t' -> SN t'. Inversion on SN t: SN_intro t H where H : forall x, t --> x -> SN x. Apply H to the step. 

Now the fundamental theorem:
```
Lemma fundamental : forall Gamma t T, has_type Gamma t T ->
  forall sigma, env_sem Gamma sigma -> LR T (subst sigma t) /\ lc (subst sigma t).
```
Actually since LR includes lc, the second component is redundant if LR includes lc at all types. Wait, LR includes lc as a conjunct at Bool and arrow. So LR T (subst sigma t) implies lc. Lemma app_LR : forall A B t u, LR (A->B) t -> LR A u -> LR B (app t u).

  intros A B.

The problem: `induction Hsn as [t Hred IH]` where the goal after `intros t Hsn` is `forall u, LR (A->B) t -> LR A u -> LR B (app t u)`. The induction principle requires the motive P x := forall u, LR (A->B) x -> LR A u -> LR B (app x u). Since t appears in the goal and Hsn : SN t, `induction Hsn` will try to generalize t. It should work: Coq's `induction Hsn as [t Hred IH]` gives goal `forall u, LR (A->B) t -> LR A u -> LR B (app t u)` and IH : forall t', step t t' -> forall u, LR (A->B) t' -> LR A u -> LR B (app t' u). I believe this works because Hsn : SN t and the goal depends on t; induction on Hsn will generalize the goal over t. Actually, Coq's induction (destruct-like) on an inductive hypothesis whose indices involve local variables: it will abstract the conclusion over t, then apply the induction principle. Yes, this is standard.

So Option B:
```
Fixpoint LR (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => strongly_normalizing t /\ locally_closed t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => strongly_normalizing t /\ locally_closed t /\
      forall u, LR T1 u -> LR T2 (tm_app t u)
  end.
```

When we do induction on Hty : has_type Gamma t T, the induction principle has a case:
```
forall (L : list atom) (Gamma : context) (T1 T2 : ty) (t1 : tm),
  (forall x : atom, ~ In x L -> has_type (update Gamma x T1) (open t1 (fvar x)) T2) ->

```
Wait, the IH for a premise of function type `forall x, ~ In x L -> has_type ...`: Coq generates `forall x (H : ~ In x L), P ...` (the induction hypothesis is under the binders). So IH : forall x (Hx : ~ In x L), P (update Gamma x T1) (open t1 (fvar x)) T2. Where P is the motive, e.g., P Gamma t T := forall sigma, env_sem Gamma sigma -> LR T (subst sigma t). So IH x Hx : forall sigma, env_sem (update Gamma x T1) sigma -> LR T2 (subst sigma (open t1 (fvar x))). 

But note: induction on Hty requires generalizing sigma (the motive includes sigma). We should set up:
```
Lemma fundamental : forall Gamma t T, has_type Gamma t T ->
  forall sigma, env_sem Gamma sigma -> LR T (subst sigma t).
Proof.
  intros Gamma t T Hty.
  induction Hty; intros sigma Henv.
```
When we do `induction Hty` with the goal `forall sigma, env_sem Gamma sigma -> ...`, Coq generalizes appropriately and the IH will be `forall sigma, env_sem ... -> LR ...`. I think `induction Hty` where Hty : has_type Gamma t T and the goal mentions Gamma, t, T will work if we `intros` sigma after; the motive is fun Gamma t T => forall sigma, env_sem Gamma sigma -> LR T (subst sigma t). Coq infers this automatically from the goal after intros. Yes.

```

- Bool: LR Bool t = SN t ∧ lc t. Have both. (Note: lc is called locally_closed = lc_at 0.) ✓

  Have SN, lc. Need forall u. Given Hu.

    - lc (app t u): locally_closed app = lc t ∧ lc u; lc u from P2 at A (Hu). But P2 at A is a lemma proven by induction on type; A is a subterm of A->B = T, so available in the induction on T? If P4 is proven by induction on T and calls P1/P2, those are proven separately (before). P1/P2 are proven by their own induction on T. So P4 can use P1 and P2 at subterms freely as long as they're already established lemmas. ✓
    - ~ value (app t u): prove `app_not_value`.
    - SN (app t u): SN_intro; given w with app t u --> w, need SN w. Invert the step:
        ST_AppAbs: t = tm_abs T0 s, value u(locally), and locally_closed (tm_abs T0 s); then value t (constructor v_abs with that lc) — contradiction with ~value t. ✓

        ST_App2: value t — contradiction. ✓
    - all reducts LR B: same inversion, using Hred and P1 at B for SN. We can combine the SN proof and the all-reducts-LR proof: first show `forall w, app t u --> w -> LR B w`, then SN via SN_intro using P1. ✓
    Then IH_B yields LR B (app t u). ✓
  ✓

     max_list : list atom -> atom := fold_right Nat.max 0.
     Lemma in_max_list : forall l x, In x l -> x <= max_list l. Induction l; simpl; intros; destruct H as [-> | H]; lia.

     Also might need y fresh w.r.t something? For LR_fvar/stuck we use an arbitrary y (any atom works), so no freshness needed.
   
2. value/lc/step lemmas:
   - value_lc : value v -> locally_closed v. destruct v: v_abs has lc premise; v_true/v_false constructors.
   - app_not_value : ~ value (tm_app t1 t2). intro H; inversion H.
   - abs_no_step : forall T s t', ~ (tm_abs T s --> t'). intro H; inversion H.

Similarly for fvar.
   - lc_step : lc t -> t --> t' -> lc t'. Proof by inversion on step:
     ST_AppAbs: need lc (open t v) from lc (app (abs T t) v). lc of app = lc (abs T t) ∧ lc v; lc (abs T t) = lc_at 1 t. Apply lc_open_rec with k=0: lc_at 1 t -> lc_at 0 v -> lc_at 0 (open_rec 0 v t) = lc (open t v). ✓
     ST_App1: lc (app t1 t2) → lc t1, lc t2; IH (t1 --> t1') gives lc t1'; lc app t1' t2 ✓. But the step inversion gives Hstep : t1 --> t1' and Hlc2 : locally_closed t2. Need IH applied to lc t1.
     ST_App2: lc (app v1 t2) → lc v1, lc t2; IH gives lc t2'; lc app v1 t2' ✓.
   - SN_step.
   - SN_intro / SN_ind.

Before writing code, let me think about whether LR_back's use of LR at type in the ST_App1 case is sound with respect to the induction structure. LR_back is proven by induction on T:
```
Lemma LR_back : forall T t, lc t -> ~ value t -> SN t ->
  (forall t', t --> t' -> LR T t') -> LR T t.
Proof.
  induction T as [| A B IHA IHB]; simpl; intros t Hlc Hnv Hsn Hred.
  - split; assumption.
  - split; [exact Hsn | split; [exact Hlc |]].
    intros u Hu.
    apply IHB.  (* backward at B, subterm *)
    + (* lc app *) split; [exact Hlc | apply LR_lc with A; exact Hu].
    + (* ~ value app *) apply app_not_value.
    + (* SN app *) apply SN_intro. intros w Hstep. 
      (* need SN w; show LR B w and apply LR_sn *)
      ...
    + (* all reducts LR B *) intros w Hstep. ...
```
Wait, IHB is the induction hypothesis for B: forall t, lc t -> ~value t -> SN t -> (forall t', t-->t' -> LR B t') -> LR B t. Yes because B is a subterm of (A->B)? In the `induction T as [| A B IHA IHB]` syntax, T is a ty; the arrow case gives A, B, IHA : forall t, ... LR A ..., IHB : forall t, ... LR B ... . ✓
But careful: the induction principle for ty: `ty_ind : forall P, P Ty_Bool -> (forall t1, P t1 -> forall t2, P t2 -> P (Ty_Arrow t1 t2)) -> forall t, P t`. So `induction T as [| A B IHA IHB]` gives IHA : P A, IHB : P B where P T := forall t, lc t -> ... -> LR T t. Good.

Hmm, but in the arrow case of LR_back, I use `IHA`? Not needed. For the reducts of app t u: ST_App1 uses Hred (LR (A->B) t'), then the arrow component of that LR gives LR B (app t' u) for any LR A u (Option B) ✓. No IHA needed. We need LR_sn and LR_lc, which are standalone.

Also the SN proof needs each reduct w to be SN: from LR B w via LR_sn. So let's first prove `forall w, app t u --> w -> LR B w`, then SN via SN_intro. But there's a wrinkle: to prove SN(app t u) via SN_intro we need `forall w, app t u --> w -> SN w`, which we get from `forall w, ... -> LR B w` plus LR_sn. Fine.

But in the ST_AppAbs case of the reducts analysis, we derive contradiction from ~value t using v_abs with lc t. Inversion on the step gives t = tm_abs T0 s and locally_closed (tm_abs T0 s) and value u. So value t follows. ✓

One subtlety: In LR_back's arrow case, when proving the reducts-LR property for app t u, the ST_App2 case requires value t: contradiction directly (Hnv). ST_AppAbs also gives value t. ST_App1: t --> t', lc u; Hred gives LR (A->B) t'; arrow component needs LR A u ✓.

Great.

Now the "all-arguments" arrow component in Option B: does it break LR_sn/LR_lc? No.

Does it break LR_step (forward closure)? LR (A->B) t' requires forall u, LR A u -> LR B (app t' u). Given u, Hu. We have (from t's component) LR B (app t u). And app t u --> app t' u via ST_App1 Hstep (lc u from LR_lc Hu). Forward closure at B gives LR B (app t' u) ✓. No value premise needed, even simpler.

Now let's reconsider the fundamental theorem T_App: LR (T1->T2) t1' (where t1' = subst sigma t1) gives forall u, LR T1 u -> LR T2 (app t1' u), apply to t2' with LR T1 t2' ✓. 

Now think about whether LR is "too strong" — is tm_true LR at arrow types? Option B: LR (A->B) true requires forall u, LR A u -> LR B (app true u). app true u: stuck unless u steps (ST_App2 with value true and u --> u'). If u steps, app true u --> app true u'. And LR B (app true u) would need... we can prove by P4/SN induction on u: app true u is lc, not value, SN (reducts from u steps handled by induction, no beta since true ≠ abs), so LR_back at B gives it — but wait, we need SN(app true u) first. SN via SN_intro: reducts are app true u' (u --> u'), and by induction on SN u (since u is LR hence SN), LR B (app true u'). Hmm, this shows LR arrow true holds; fine, it's permissive but consistent. The fundamental theorem only needs to build LR for actual terms; permissiveness is okay as long as the lemmas go through.

But hold on: LR_back at type B for app true u requires "forall t', app true u --> t' -> LR B t'"; the reducts are app true u' from ST_App2. To get LR B (app true u'), we could again use LR_back with a nested SN induction on u. In LR_back's proof, we had Hred for t=true which is vacuous, and u arbitrary LR. The reducts of app true u: ST_App2 with value true, u --> u'. We need LR B (app true u'). This is NOT covered by any induction hypothesis! We don't have induction on u in LR_back. Uh oh. This is the "stuck value" problem: t = true is a value of type A->B (permissively), and app true u can step when u steps.

So LR_back as stated might be unprovable for arrow types when t is a value (true/false) that isn't an abs! Let's re-examine: In LR_back, t has ~value t as hypothesis. Oh wait! LR_back assumes ~ value t. So t = true is excluded. Phew. Then ST_App2 (requires value t) is impossible, and ST_AppAbs (requires t = abs, which with lc gives value t) impossible. So only ST_App1 remains. Great. So LR_back is fine.

But then in the fundamental theorem's abs case, we apply LR_back to app (abs s) u. Is app ... not a value? Yes, apps are never values. So the ~value premise holds. The reducts include ST_AppAbs (t=abs, u value) → open s u (handled by H_s) and ST_App2 (value abs, u --> u') → app (abs s) u' (handled by IH from SN_ind on u). ✓. So the abs case needs the SN induction on u, as planned. Good.

But wait: in the abs case, LR_back is applied at type T2 to the term app (abs s) u with the hypothesis "forall w, app (abs s) u --> w -> LR T2 w". This includes the ST_AppAbs reduct open s u, which LR_back does NOT need to handle internally (it's a hypothesis). ✓ Good — LR_back's proof only uses the reduct hypothesis, not SN of u or anything. 

Now, another concern: In the fundamental theorem's abs case, for LR (T1->T2) (abs s) we need forall u, LR T1 u -> LR T2 (app (abs s) u). We prove it by SN_ind on u, and inside use LR_back at T2 on app (abs s) u. LR_back's hypotheses:
- lc(app): lc(abs s) ∧ lc u. lc(abs s) = lc_at 1 s which we derived from H_s at fvar y. But hold on: H_s required LR T1 u for arbitrary u. For deriving lc_at 1 s we used u = fvar y, giving LR T2 (open s (fvar y)) → lc. But H_s itself (forall u, LR T1 u -> LR T2 (open s u)) is derived via the typing IH for each u. Deriving it for u = fvar y is fine. ✓
- ~value(app) ✓.
- SN(app): SN_intro: forall reducts w, SN w. Reducts: ST_AppAbs → open s u, LR T2 from H_s u Hu (note: requires LR T1 u which we have, and H_s works for ALL u, not just values — this is exactly why Option B is nice); ST_App2 → app (abs s) u' with u --> u'; IH from SN_ind: P u' = LR T1 u' -> LR T2 (app (abs s) u'), and LR T1 u' from forward closure at T1 of Hu. ✓; ST_App1 impossible. 
- all reducts LR T2: same analysis.

Wait, there's a subtle circularity: the SN_ind property P u := LR T1 u -> LR T2 (app (abs s) u) — to prove P u we use SN_intro on app (abs s) u, whose reduct app (abs s) u' gets LR T2 via IH (P u') applied to LR T1 u'. Then SN w via LR_sn. Fine, no circularity: the SN derivation of app (abs s) u depends on SN of app (abs s) u' which comes from IH at u' (whose proof uses the SN derivation of u' — available from SN_ind's premise Hred2). Good.

Also, H_s : forall u, LR T1 u -> LR T2 (open s u) — is this exactly what the typing IH gives? The typing IH gives, for each fresh x: forall sigma, env_sem (update Gamma x T1) sigma -> LR T2 (subst sigma (open t1 (fvar x))). For any u with LR T1 u, set sigma_u := upd sigma x u. Then subst sigma_u (open t1 (fvar x)) = open (subst sigma_u t1) (subst sigma_u (fvar x)) = open (subst sigma_u t1) u. Since x ∉ fv t1, subst sigma_u t1 = subst sigma t1 = s. So = open s u. ✓

And lc_at 1 s comes from H_s at u=fvar y: LR T2 (open s (fvar y)) → lc_at 0 (open_rec 0 (fvar y) s). Note subst sigma_u (fvar x) = sigma_u x = fvar y. ✓. Then lc_open_inv with k=0 and u=fvar y: from lc_at 0 (open_rec 0 (fvar y) s) conclude lc_at 1 s. ✓ (no lc hypothesis on fvar needed).

Hmm, wait: we also need s = subst sigma t1 to be such that lc_at 1 s implies lc (tm_abs T1 s) = lc_at 0 (tm_abs T1 s) = lc_at 1 s. lc_abs constructor: lc_at k (tm_abs T t) from lc_at (S k) t. With k=0: lc_at 1 t. ✓

So everything hinges on choosing a fresh x for the T_Abs premise. Need: x ∉ L and x ∉ fv t1. L is a list atom from the typing rule. fv t1 a list. Choose x := fresh (L ++ fv t1).

Hmm, subtle point: `subst sigma_u t1 = subst sigma t1` requires x ∉ fv t1 ✓. But also, `subst sigma_u (open t1 (fvar x)) = open (subst sigma_u t1) (subst sigma_u (fvar x))` — the lemma subst_open_rec. Does subst_open_rec hold unconditionally? Yes.

```
Definition env_sem (Gamma : context) (sigma : atom -> tm) : Prop :=
  forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> LR T (sigma x).
```
In T_Abs: env_sem (update Gamma x T1) sigma_u from env_sem Gamma sigma and LR T1 u:
```
Lemma env_sem_update : forall Gamma sigma x T v,
  env_sem Gamma sigma -> LR T v -> env_sem (update Gamma x T) (upd sigma x v).
Proof.
  intros Gamma sigma x T v Henv Hv y U Hy.
  unfold update in Hy. destruct (Nat.eqb x y) eqn:E.
  - (* x = y *) inversion Hy; subst. unfold upd. rewrite E. exact Hv.
    Hmm: update Gamma x T y = if x =? y then Some T else Gamma y. If true, then Some T = Some U → T = U. Need LR U (upd sigma x v y) = LR U v (since eqb x y = true). So rewrite E in upd; exact (eq_rect ...).
  - (* x ≠ y *) Hy : Gamma y = Some U. unfold upd; rewrite E. apply Henv; exact Hy.
Qed.
```
Careful with dependent rewriting. Let's write:
```
  unfold update in Hy. destruct (Nat.eqb x y) eqn:E.
  + inversion Hy; subst U. unfold upd. rewrite E. exact Hv.
  + unfold upd. rewrite E. apply Henv. exact Hy.
```
After inversion Hy, Hy : Some T = Some U → inversion gives T = U? Hy is `(if ... then Some T else Gamma y) = Some U`; after destruct, in the true branch Hy : Some T = Some U. `inversion Hy; subst` should give U = T (or T = U) and clear. Then goal LR U (upd sigma x v y); unfold upd; rewrite E; goal LR T v. If inversion substituted U := T, exact Hv. Let's be careful: `inversion Hy as [Heq]`? Inversion of Some T = Some U gives T = U. `injection Hy as ->`? Since Hy : Some T = Some U, `injection Hy as Heq` gives T = U. Then subst. Let's just use `inversion Hy; subst; ...`. Fine.

Also we need Nat.eqb reflection: `Nat.eqb_eq : forall x y, (x =? y) = true <-> x = y`, `Nat.eqb_neq`. With Arith.PeanoNat imported, `Nat.eqb_eq` and `Nat.eqb_neq` should be available. And `Nat.eqb` reduction: `destruct (Nat.eqb x y) eqn:E`.

Now, let's think about possible pitfalls:

(a) The `subst` Fixpoint and the `subst` tactic name conflict! Coq's `subst` tactic is named `subst`; defining a function named `subst` could shadow or confuse? In Coq, a definition named `subst` is allowed? The tactic `subst` is in Ltac; a const named subst might conflict in parsing? I recall `subst` is a tactic keyword; defining a term named `subst` might be OK but could cause issues when using the `subst` tactic. Safer to name it `subst_tm` or `tsubst`. Let's call it `subst_tm`. Similarly `open` exists already (Definition open). We'll use `open_rec`.

(b) The `fv` function name — no conflicts likely. `max_list` maybe conflicts with List's `list_max`? We'll name it `max_atoms`.

(c) Custom scopes/notations interfere when writing terms? The file defines notations in custom entries; if we write `tm_app t1 t2` directly it should parse fine outside the stlc_scope? The scope `stlc_scope` is open. The notations like "x y" for app are in custom entry stlc_tm, so they only apply inside `<{ ... }>`. Direct constructors should be fine. But `true` notation is scoped: `Notation "'true'" := tm_true (in custom stlc_tm at level 0) : stlc_scope.` The scope is open but the notation is only for the custom entry, so bare `true` refers to Bool.true. We'll use tm_true directly.

(d) The `has_type` notation `<{ Gamma |-- t \in T }>` might parse oddly; in lemmas we can use `has_type Gamma t T` directly.

(e) In the induction on has_type, the constructors have implicit notation types; using `induction Hty` should give cases.

Let me plan the file additions (inserted before `Theorem normalization`):

```coq
(** Substitution of free variables (atoms). *)
Fixpoint subst_tm (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst_tm sigma t1) (subst_tm sigma t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst_tm sigma t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  end.

Definition upd (sigma : atom -> tm) (x : atom) (v : tm) : atom -> tm :=
  fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then v else sigma y.

Fixpoint fv (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => nil
  | tm_fvar x => cons x nil
  | tm_app t1 t2 => app (fv t1) (fv t2)
  | tm_abs _ t1 => fv t1
  | tm_true => nil
  | tm_false => nil
  end.
```

Wait, `app` is List.app? `List.app`. But `app` might conflict with tm_app? The name `app` from List is imported; `tm_app` is a constructor. In `fv`, use `List.app` or `++`. We have `From Stdlib Require Import Lists.List.` so `++` notation for list app is available. Use `(fv t1) ++ (fv t2)`.

Lemma subst_tm_upd_notin : forall sigma x v t,

Use `exfalso; apply H; left; symmetry; exact ...`. Then no goal. If false, goal subst_tm ... = ... becomes `sigma a = sigma a`? unfold upd; rewrite E; reflexivity.
Actually after unfolding upd and destructing, the goal for fvar a is `(if x =? a then v else sigma a) = sigma a`; destruct makes it either `v = sigma a` or `sigma a = sigma a`. In the true branch, derive contradiction. So:

```
Wait, `subst` here is the tactic — fine since we named our function subst_tm. Good.

For app: goal after simpl is `tm_app (subst_tm (upd sigma x v) t1) (subst_tm (upd sigma x v) t2) = tm_app (subst_tm sigma t1) (subst_tm sigma t2)`. H : ~ In x (fv t1 ++ fv t2). `apply in_app_iff` gives In x l1 \/ In x l2? The lemma is `in_app_iff : forall (l l' : list A) (a : A), In a (l ++ l') <-> In a l \/ In a l'`. `apply H` where H : ~ In x (l1++l2) and goal `In x (l1++l2)`; then `apply in_app_iff; auto`. So: `intro Hc; apply H; apply in_app_iff; auto.` Actually `apply H; apply in_app_iff; left; exact Hc` — the `;` applies to all goals; let's be explicit.

Freshness:
```coq
Fixpoint max_atoms (l : list atom) : atom :=
  match l with
  | nil => 0
  | cons x xs => Nat.max x (max_atoms xs)
  end.

Lemma in_max_atoms : forall l x, In x l -> x <= max_atoms l.
Proof. induction l as [| y ys IH]; simpl; intros x H; [contradiction|].
  destruct H as [-> | H].
  - apply Nat.le_max_l.
  - apply Nat.le_trans with (max_atoms ys); [apply IH; exact H | apply Nat.le_max_r].
Qed.

Lemma fresh_atom : forall l : list atom, exists x, ~ In x l.
Proof. intro l. exists (S (max_atoms l)). intro H. apply in_max_atoms in H. lia. Qed.
```
Need `lia` — from Lia library. The file imports Arith.PeanoNat but not Lia. We can Require Import Lia. Or use `apply Nat.nlt_ge`? `in_max_atoms` gives x <= m; H gives x = S m; so S m <= m, contradiction: `apply Nat.succ_le_iff in ...`? Simplest: `Require Import Lia.` (Lia is a standard Rocq library, no axioms). Or use `omega`. `omega` is in Omega (deprecated?) or `lia`. Let's import Lia.

Better: `intros k j t Hle Hlc. revert k j Hle Hlc. induction t; intros; inversion Hlc; subst; ...` Hmm, induction on t with k,j generalized. Let me write:
```
Lemma lc_at_mono : forall k j t, k <= j -> lc_at k t -> lc_at j t.
Proof.
  intros k j t. revert k j. induction t; intros k j Hle Hlc; inversion Hlc; subst; constructor; auto.
  - (* bvar *) apply Nat.lt_le_trans with k; assumption.

Now LR definition:
```
Fixpoint LR (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => strongly_normalizing t /\ locally_closed t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 =>
      strongly_normalizing t /\ locally_closed t /\
      (forall u, LR T1 u -> LR T2 (tm_app t u))
  end.
```
Coq requires the Fixpoint to be structurally recursive on T — LR T1 u and LR T2 (tm_app t u) — the recursive calls are on T1 and T2 which are subterms of Ty_Arrow T1 T2. However, `LR T1 u` appears in a negative position? No, it's a positive occurrence in a forall premise: `forall u, LR T1 u -> LR T2 (...)`. Coq's guard checker: recursive calls in the body must be on structural subterms; both T1 and T2 are. The occurrence in the domain of an arrow (premise) is allowed? The guard condition allows recursive occurrences anywhere in the body as applications to subterms, I believe. However, there's the issue of "non-uniform" parameters? t changes: LR T1 u where u is a subterm... The fixpoint is on T, with t as a parameter that changes across recursive calls (t becomes tm_app t u). Coq's guard checker allows recursive calls where the recursive argument is a subterm of the original recursive argument (T1/T2 < T); the other arguments can be anything. Yes, this should be accepted.

Wait, actually there's a subtlety: the recursive call `LR T1 u` occurs in the *domain* of a forall, which is a negative position? For Fixpoint guard checking, negative occurrences of the fixpoint are not allowed? The guard checker only checks recursive calls, not polarity, for inductives? Hmm, for a Fixpoint returning Prop, recursive calls in negative positions are allowed as long as they're on structurally smaller arguments? I recall Coq allows recursive definitions in Prop with negative occurrences (e.g., accessibility predicates, or `Fixpoint even n := match n with ... end`). The guard condition is about recursive calls being on subterms. For example, the standard logical relation definition:

```
Fixpoint LR (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Bool => ...
  | Arrow T1 T2 => forall u, LR T1 u -> LR T2 (app t u)
  end.
```
This is a common definition and Coq accepts it (e.g., in many developments). Yes.

But one concern: `t` is a parameter that changes: recursive call LR T2 (tm_app t u) — the recursive argument is T2 (smaller), and the non-recursive argument changes; that's fine since the fixpoint is only on T (T is the decreasing argument as it appears in the match). Actually in the definition `Fixpoint LR (T : ty) (t : tm)`, Coq determines the decreasing argument from the match: T. Recursive calls must have the decreasing argument structurally smaller: T1 and T2 are. ✓.

LR_back:
```
Lemma LR_back : forall T t,
  locally_closed t ->

  strongly_normalizing t ->

```
Lemma step_app_inv : forall t u w,
  tm_app t u --> w ->

Now, the final theorem:
```
Theorem normalization : forall t T,
  <{ empty |-- t \in T }> ->
  strongly_normalizing t.
Proof.
  intros t T Hty.
  pose proof (fundamental _ _ _ Hty (fun x => tm_fvar x) (fun x U H => ...)) as HLR.
```
env_sem empty (fun x => tm_fvar x): forall x T, empty x = Some T -> LR T (fvar x). empty x = None, so the premise is false: `intros x U H; unfold empty in H; discriminate H.`
Then HLR : LR T (subst_tm (fun x => tm_fvar x) t). Rewrite subst_tm_id in HLR. Apply LR_sn. ✓

```coq
(** Substitution of free variables. *)

Fixpoint subst_tm (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst_tm sigma t1) (subst_tm sigma t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst_tm sigma t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  end.

Definition upd (sigma : atom -> tm) (x : atom) (v : tm) : atom -> tm :=
  fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then v else sigma y.

Fixpoint fv (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => nil
  | tm_fvar x => x :: nil
  | tm_app t1 t2 => fv t1 ++ fv t2
  | tm_abs _ t1 => fv t1
  | tm_true => nil
  | tm_false => nil
  end.

Lemma upd_same : forall sigma x v, upd sigma x v x = v.
Proof. intros. unfold upd. rewrite Nat.eqb_refl. reflexivity. Qed.

Lemma upd_neq : forall sigma x y v, x <> y -> upd sigma x v y = sigma y.
Proof. intros sigma x y v H. unfold upd. destruct (Nat.eqb x y) eqn:E.
  - apply Nat.eqb_eq in E. contradiction.
  - reflexivity. Qed.

Lemma subst_tm_id : forall t, subst_tm (fun x => tm_fvar x) t = t.
Proof. induction t; simpl; congruence. Qed.
```

Actually `simpl` will beta-reduce. Then goals become reflexive or follow from IH. `induction t; simpl; congruence` — for fvar, simpl gives tm_fvar a = tm_fvar a, reflexivity; congruence closes. Lemma lc_at_mono : forall k j t, k <= j -> lc_at k t -> lc_at j t.
Proof.
  intros k j t Hle. revert k j Hle. induction t; intros k j Hle Hlc; inversion Hlc; subst.

  - apply lc_fvar.
  - apply lc_app; [apply IHt1 | apply IHt2]; assumption.

  - apply lc_true.
  - apply lc_false.
Qed.

Lemma lc_open_rec : forall s k u,
  lc_at (S k) s -> lc_at k u -> lc_at k (open_rec k u s).

  - apply lc_false.
Qed.
```

- Nat.eqb_neq : forall x y, (x =? y) = false <-> x <> y

Lemma lc_open_inv : forall s k u,

  - apply lc_true.
  - apply lc_false.
Qed.
```

```coq
(** ** Substitution of free variables *)

Fixpoint subst_tm (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst_tm sigma t1) (subst_tm sigma t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst_tm sigma t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  end.

Definition upd (sigma : atom -> tm) (x : atom) (v : tm) : atom -> tm :=
  fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then v else sigma y.

Lemma subst_tm_fvar : forall sigma x, subst_tm sigma (tm_fvar x) = sigma x.
Proof. reflexivity. Qed.

Lemma upd_same : forall sigma x v, upd sigma x v x = v.
Proof. intros. unfold upd. rewrite Nat.eqb_refl. reflexivity. Qed.

Lemma upd_neq : forall sigma x y v, x <> y -> upd sigma x v y = sigma y.
Proof.
  intros sigma x y v H. unfold upd.
  destruct (Nat.eqb x y) eqn:E.
  - apply Nat.eqb_eq in E. contradiction.
  - reflexivity.
Qed.

Fixpoint fv (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => nil
  | tm_fvar x => x :: nil
  | tm_app t1 t2 => fv t1 ++ fv t2
  | tm_abs _ t1 => fv t1
  | tm_true => nil
  | tm_false => nil
  end.

Lemma subst_tm_id : forall t, subst_tm (fun x => tm_fvar x) t = t.
Proof. induction t; simpl; congruence. Qed.

Lemma subst_tm_open_rec : forall sigma t u k,
  subst_tm sigma (open_rec k u t) =
  open_rec k (subst_tm sigma u) (subst_tm sigma t).
Proof.
  intros sigma t. induction t; intros u k; simpl.
  - destruct (Nat.eqb k n); reflexivity.
  - reflexivity.
  - rewrite IHt1, IHt2. reflexivity.
  - rewrite IHt. reflexivity.
  - reflexivity.
  - reflexivity.
Qed.
```

Hmm wait: in the abs case `rewrite IHt` — but IHt after `induction t` is the IH for the body? The abs constructor's argument is t1; `induction t as [n | a | t1 IHt1 t2 IHt2 | T t1 IHt1 | | ]`. Coq auto-names IHt1 for the abs body maybe. In the bullet structure, I referred to IHt/IHt1/IHt2 by guessed names. Since induction names them automatically as IHt1, IHt2, IHt1 (duplicate?) — Coq would name them IHt1 (for app's first), IHt2 (app's second), IHt1 (abs body?) conflict resolved as IHt1, IHt2, IHt3 maybe. This guessing is risky. Better to explicitly name with `induction t as [n | a | t1 IH1 t2 IH2 | T t1 IH1 | | ]`. In Coq 9, the syntax for multiple arguments in a constructor: `| t1 IH1 t2 IH2`? The induction pattern for tm: 
- tm_bvar : nat -> tm: pattern `n` names the nat, no IH.
- tm_fvar : atom -> tm: `a`.
- tm_app : tm -> tm -> tm: `t1 IH1 t2 IH2`.
- tm_abs : ty -> tm -> tm: `T t1 IH`.
- tm_true, tm_false: nothing.
So: `induction t as [n | a | t1 IH1 t2 IH2 | T t1 IH | |]`.

Similarly for other inductions, name explicitly.

```coq
Lemma subst_tm_open_rec : forall sigma t u k,
  subst_tm sigma (open_rec k u t) =
  open_rec k (subst_tm sigma u) (subst_tm sigma t).
Proof.
  intros sigma t. induction t as [n | a | t1 IH1 t2 IH2 | T t1 IH | |];
    intros u k; simpl.
  - destruct (Nat.eqb k n); reflexivity.
  - reflexivity.
  - rewrite IH1, IH2. reflexivity.
  - rewrite IH. reflexivity.
  - reflexivity.
  - reflexivity.
Qed.
```

```coq
Lemma subst_tm_upd_notin : forall sigma x v t,
  ~ In x (fv t) -> subst_tm (upd sigma x v) t = subst_tm sigma t.
Proof.
  intros sigma x v t. induction t as [n | a | t1 IH1 t2 IH2 | T t1 IH | |];
    simpl; intros H.
  - reflexivity.
  - unfold upd. destruct (Nat.eqb x a) eqn:E.
    + exfalso. apply H. apply Nat.eqb_eq in E. subst a. left. reflexivity.
    + reflexivity.
  - assert (H1 : ~ In x (fv t1)) by (intro Hc; apply H; apply in_app_iff; left; exact Hc).
    assert (H2 : ~ In x (fv t2)) by (intro Hc; apply H; apply in_app_iff; right; exact Hc).
    rewrite IH1 by exact H1. rewrite IH2 by exact H2. reflexivity.
  - rewrite IH by exact H. reflexivity.
  - reflexivity.
  - reflexivity.
Qed.
```

Now freshness:
```coq
Fixpoint max_atoms (l : list atom) : atom :=
  match l with
  | nil => 0
  | x :: xs => Nat.max x (max_atoms xs)
  end.

Lemma in_max_atoms : forall l x, In x l -> x <= max_atoms l.
Proof.
  induction l as [| y ys IH]; simpl; intros x H.
  - contradiction.
  - destruct H as [-> | H].
    + apply Nat.le_max_l.
    + apply Nat.le_trans with (max_atoms ys); [apply IH; exact H | apply Nat.le_max_r].
Qed.

Lemma fresh_atom : forall l : list atom, exists x, ~ In x l.
Proof.
  intros l. exists (S (max_atoms l)). intro H.
  apply in_max_atoms in H. apply Nat.succ_le_iff in H? 
```

lc_open_rec with explicit patterns on term induction:
```
Lemma lc_open_rec : forall s k u,
  lc_at (S k) s -> lc_at k u -> lc_at k (open_rec k u s).
Proof.
  intros s. induction s as [n | a | s1 IH1 s2 IH2 | T s1 IH | |];
    intros k u Hlc Hu; simpl.
  - destruct (Nat.eqb k n) eqn:E.
    + apply Nat.eqb_eq in E. subst n. exact Hu.
    + apply lc_bvar. inversion Hlc as [k' i Hi | | | | |]; subst. 
      apply Nat.eqb_neq in E. lia.
  - apply lc_fvar.
  - inversion Hlc as [k' i Hi | k' x | k' a b H1 H2 | k' T' b H1 | k' | k']; subst.
    apply lc_app; [apply IH1 | apply IH2]; assumption.
  - inversion Hlc as [k' i Hi | k' x | k' a b H1 H2 | k' T' b H1 | k' | k']; subst.
    apply lc_abs. apply IH with (k := S k).
    + assumption.
    + apply lc_at_mono with (k := k); [lia | exact Hu].
  - apply lc_true.
  - apply lc_false.
Qed.
```
Hmm, inversion `as` patterns for lc_at: lc_at k (tm_bvar n) etc. The `as` pattern for inversion lists the arguments of the constructor case. For lc_bvar : forall k i, i < k -> lc_at k (tm_bvar i): inversion of Hlc : lc_at (S k) (tm_bvar n) unifies; the pattern `[k' i Hi | ...]` names k', i, Hi. But inversion also might generate an equation for the index? Since the indices unify by constructor (tm_bvar i = tm_bvar n → i = n), inversion substitutes. I think the pattern `as [k' i Hi | ...]` works, but the number of names must match exactly what inversion generates, which may include equalities. Risky.

Alternative: avoid inversion patterns by using `inversion Hlc; subst` and then use hypothesis names generated automatically in a robust way with `assumption`/`lia` and `match`. In the bvar case: after `inversion Hlc; subst`, we have a hypothesis (say H) : n < S k. Goal n < k. `apply Nat.eqb_neq in E; lia` — lia can find H automatically from context! So no need to name it. Similarly for app: after inversion, hypotheses lc_at k s1, lc_at k s2 exist; `apply lc_app; [apply IH1 | apply IH2]; assumption` — assumption should find them (it searches the context). ✓
For abs: after inversion, hypothesis lc_at (S k) s1 exists; `apply IH with (k := S k)`? Hmm, IH : forall k u, lc_at (S k) s1 -> lc_at k u -> lc_at k (open_rec k u s1). Goal after apply lc_abs: lc_at (S k) (open_rec (S k) u s1). Apply IH with (k := S k): subgoals lc_at (S (S k)) s1 and lc_at (S k) u. `assumption` should find the inverted hypothesis if it's exactly lc_at (S (S k)) s1. Let's check: Hlc : lc_at (S k) (tm_abs T s1) → inversion gives lc_at (S (S k)) s1. ✓ `assumption` finds it. And lc_at (S k) u from Hu by mono. ✓

So use `inversion Hlc; subst` without as-patterns and `assumption`. Good.

But there's a risk: `inversion Hlc; subst` might not substitute the index in a useful way, or might produce weird stuff. Let's compile and see.

Lemma SN_ind : forall (P : tm -> Prop),
  (forall t, (forall t', t --> t' -> P t') -> P t) ->
  forall t, strongly_normalizing t -> P t.
Proof.
  intros P H t Hsn. induction Hsn as [t Hred IH]. apply H. exact IH.
Qed.
```

`step_app_cases`:
```
Lemma step_app_cases : forall t v w,
  tm_app t v --> w ->
  (exists T s, t = tm_abs T s /\ w = open s v) \/
  (exists t', t --> t' /\ w = tm_app t' v) \/
  (exists v', value t /\ v --> v' /\ w = tm_app t v').
Proof.
  intros t v w H. inversion H; subst.
  - left. exists T. exists t0. split; reflexivity.
  - right. left. exists t1'. split; [assumption | reflexivity].
  - right. right. exists t2'. split; [assumption | split; [assumption | reflexivity]].
Qed.
```
The variable names T, t0, t1', t2' depend on inversion-generated names — risky. Use eexists and eassumption:
```
  - left. eexists. eexists. split; reflexivity.
  - right. left. eexists. split; [eassumption | reflexivity].
  - right. right. eexists. split; [eassumption | split; [eassumption | reflexivity]].
```
`eexists` creates an evar; split; reflexivity should instantiate? In case 1: goal exists T s, t = tm_abs T s /\ w = open s v. After inversion ST_AppAbs, t is (substituted to) tm_abs T0 s0, v = v0, w = open s0 v0. Goal: exists T s, tm_abs T0 s0 = tm_abs T s /\ open s0 v0 = open s v0. `eexists; eexists; split; reflexivity` — the evars T,s unified by reflexivity. Should work.
Case 2: goal exists t', t1 --> t' /\ tm_app t1' t2 = tm_app t' t2? After inversion, source t v = app t1 t2 so t := t1, v := t2, w := app t1' t2. Goal: exists t', t1 --> t' /\ app t1' t2 = app t' t2. `eexists; split; [eassumption | reflexivity]`. ✓
Case 3: exists v', value t /\ v --> v' /\ app t v' = app t v'. Wait, source app v1 t2 = app t v → v1 = t, t2 = v; w = app v1 t2' = app t t2'. Goal: exists v', value t /\ v --> v' /\ app t t2' = app t v'. `eexists; split; [eassumption | split; [eassumption | reflexivity]]`. ✓

Definition env_sem (Gamma : context) (sigma : atom -> tm) : Prop :=
  forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> LR T (sigma x).

Lemma env_sem_update : forall Gamma sigma x T v,
  env_sem Gamma sigma -> LR T v -> env_sem (update Gamma x T) (upd sigma x v).
Proof.

  unfold update in Hy. unfold upd.
  destruct (Nat.eqb x y) eqn:E.
  - apply Nat.eqb_eq in E. subst y. injection Hy as Hy'. subst U. exact Hv.
  - apply Henv. exact Hy.
Qed.

Lemma env_sem_update : forall Gamma sigma x T v,
  env_sem Gamma sigma -> LR T v -> env_sem (update Gamma x T) (upd sigma x v).

```
Lemma update_same : forall Gamma x T, update Gamma x T x = Some T.
Proof. intros. unfold update. rewrite Nat.eqb_refl. reflexivity. Qed.
Lemma update_neq : forall Gamma x y T, x <> y -> update Gamma x T y = Gamma y.
Proof. intros. unfold update. destruct (Nat.eqb x y) eqn:E; [apply Nat.eqb_eq in E; contradiction | reflexivity]. Qed.
```

Let me write the fundamental theorem:

```
Lemma fundamental : forall Gamma t T,
  has_type Gamma t T ->
  forall sigma, env_sem Gamma sigma -> LR T (subst_tm sigma t).
Proof.
  intros Gamma t T Hty.
  induction Hty; intros sigma Henv.
  - (* T_Var *) simpl. apply Henv. exact H.
  - (* T_Abs *)
    simpl.
    destruct (fresh_atom (L ++ fv t1)) as [x Hx].
    assert (HxL : ~ In x L) by (intro Hc; apply Hx; apply in_app_iff; left; exact Hc).
    assert (Hxfv : ~ In x (fv t1)) by (intro Hc; apply Hx; apply in_app_iff; right; exact Hc).
    assert (Hsub : forall u, subst_tm (upd sigma x u) (open t1 (tm_fvar x)) = open (subst_tm sigma t1) u).
    { intros u. unfold open. rewrite subst_tm_open_rec. rewrite subst_tm_fvar.
      rewrite upd_same. rewrite subst_tm_upd_notin by exact Hxfv. reflexivity. }
    assert (Hs : forall u, LR T1 u -> LR T2 (open (subst_tm sigma t1) u)).
    { intros u Hu.
      pose proof (IH x HxL (upd sigma x u)) as Hiu.
      apply Hiu. apply env_sem_update; [exact Henv | exact Hu]. }
    assert (Hlc_s : lc_at 1 (subst_tm sigma t1)).
    { pose proof (Hs (tm_fvar 0) (LR_fvar T1 0)) as H0.
      pose proof (LR_lc T2 _ H0) as H1.
      exact (lc_open_inv (subst_tm sigma t1) 0 (tm_fvar 0) H1). }
    split.
    + (* SN abs *) apply SN_intro. intros t' Hst. inversion Hst.
    + split.
      * (* lc abs *) apply lc_abs. exact Hlc_s.
      * (* application component *)
        intros u Hu.
        assert (Hsn_u : strongly_normalizing u) by (apply LR_sn with (T := T1); exact Hu).
        revert Hu.
        refine (SN_ind (fun v => LR T1 v -> LR T2 (tm_app (tm_abs T1 (subst_tm sigma t1)) v)) _ u Hsn_u).
        intros v Hred IHv Hv.
        assert (Hreds : forall w, tm_app (tm_abs T1 (subst_tm sigma t1)) v --> w -> LR T2 w).
        { intros w Hst.
          destruct (step_app_cases (tm_abs T1 (subst_tm sigma t1)) v w Hst) as
            [[T0 [s [Heq Hw]]] | [[t' [Hstep Hw]] | [v' [Hval [Hstep Hw]]]]].
          - injection Heq as HT Hs_eq. subst T0. subst s. rewrite Hw. apply Hs. exact Hv.
          - exfalso. eapply abs_not_step. eexact Hstep.
          - rewrite Hw. apply IHv.
            + exact Hstep.
            + apply LR_step with (t := v); [exact Hv | exact Hstep].
        }
        apply LR_back with (T := T2).
        * (* lc *) split; [apply lc_abs; exact Hlc_s | apply LR_lc with (T := T1); exact Hv].
        * apply app_not_value.
        * apply SN_intro. intros w Hst. apply LR_sn with (T := T2). apply Hreds. exact Hst.
        * exact Hreds.
  - (* T_App *)
    simpl. destruct (IHHty1 sigma Henv) as [Hsn [Hlc Hfun]].
    apply Hfun. apply IHHty2. exact Henv.
  - (* T_True *) simpl. split.
    + apply SN_intro. intros t' Hst. inversion Hst.
    + (* lc *) apply lc_true? locally_closed tm_true = lc_at 0 tm_true: lc_true.
      exact (lc_true 0).
  - (* T_False *) simpl. split.
    + apply SN_intro. intros t' Hst. inversion Hst.
    + exact (lc_false 0).
Qed.
```

Theorem normalization : forall t T,
